# BOOK I: FOUNDATIONS OF TIME

---

## Part 1: The Physics of Time

### 1.1 Newton's Absolute Time — The Intuition We All Start With

Before Einstein came along, time was simple. Comfortingly, confidently simple. In 1687, Isaac Newton — the great English physicist whose *Principia Mathematica* laid out the laws of motion and gravity — described time as "absolute, true, and mathematical," flowing "equably" without relation to anything external. Let's unpack those old-fashioned words, because they carry the whole idea. "Absolute" means independent — not depending on anything else. "True and mathematical" means it's the perfect, precise kind of time that clocks can only approximate. And "flowing equably" means flowing *smoothly and evenly* — every second exactly as long as every other second, everywhere, forever, no exceptions.

Picture it this way: time, for Newton, is like a great invisible river that flows through the universe at one fixed speed. Everything that has ever happened or ever will happen — every atom, every galaxy, every breakfast — floats on this river. The river doesn't speed up near a black hole or slow down on a fast spaceship. It just flows. One second on Earth is one second on Mars, one second for a photon racing across the galaxy, one second for you and one second for me. Time is the stage on which the drama of the universe plays out — but the stage itself never changes, no matter what happens on it.

And here is the thing: this is the intuition you carry in your bones. You don't feel like time behaves differently for an astronaut than it does for you. When you agree to meet a friend at 7:00 PM, you both assume you're talking about the *same* 7:00 PM — a single, shared moment out there in the world that you'll both arrive at. When we say "in 1492, Columbus sailed the ocean blue," we believe that statement points to a real moment that existed objectively — the same moment for Columbus, experiencing it, and for us, looking back. When you read that a dinosaur lived 65 million years ago, you instinctively imagine a dot on a timeline that stretches the same way for everyone. Newtonian time is so deeply baked into ordinary language and everyday thought that even now, three and a half centuries after it was overthrown at the fundamental level, we all still speak and think in Newtonian terms most of the day. It's the water we swim in.

Now, Newton was a genius, but he was honest about the limits of his own idea. He admitted that he was defining time by its *mathematical properties* — how it behaves in equations — rather than by its *essence* — what it actually is. He could describe how time flows, but not what it's made of or why it flows at all. Absolute time, in other words, was a kind of useful fiction: a coordinate system, like the grid lines on a map, that made the math work beautifully. And for about two hundred years, it did work beautifully. Planets orbited on schedule, cannonballs arced exactly as predicted, and clocks kept pace with it all — every calculation assuming one universal "now" stretched across the entire cosmos, the same everywhere, unchanging. Physics had its tidy clockwork universe, and the clockwork ran on Newton's time.

### 1.2 Einstein's Revolution — Time Is Relative

In 1905, a twenty-six-year-old patent clerk in Switzerland named Albert Einstein published a short paper that detonated this entire picture. The paper was on what we now call *special relativity*, and the explosion came from two deceptively simple starting points, called postulates. Think of postulates as the two rules Einstein refused to compromise on, the foundation stones of his new building.

**Postulate one** says: the laws of physics are the same for everyone moving at a constant velocity. "Constant velocity" means moving in a straight line at a steady speed — no accelerating, no turning. If you're on a smoothly cruising train and you drop a ball, it falls straight down to the floor, exactly as it would on the platform. There is no experiment you can do inside the train to prove it's moving, as long as the ride is smooth. Einstein said this equality of experience isn't just approximately true — it's exact and universal.

**Postulate two** is the bombshell: the speed of light in a vacuum is the same for everyone, *no matter what*. It doesn't matter how fast you're moving toward a light beam or how fast the light's source is moving away from you. Measure the speed of that light, and you will always get exactly the same number: 299,792,458 meters per second — about 670 million miles per hour. Light never, ever rushes past you faster because you're chasing it. It never creeps by slower because you're fleeing it.

Now stop and feel how strange postulate two is, because everything in daily life disagrees with it. If you're in a car doing 60 mph and you throw a ball forward at 20 mph, common sense says the ball moves at 80 mph relative to the road. Speeds add. That's how the world works — except for light. If you shine a flashlight out the window while driving 60 mph, the light does *not* travel at the speed of light plus 60 mph. It travels at exactly the speed of light. The beam's speed is utterly indifferent to your car's speed. If this feels unfair — like the universe is breaking its own addition rules — good. That's exactly the reaction physicists had. And the conclusion Einstein drew was that if light refuses to negotiate, *something else has to give*. That something else is time itself.

#### Time Dilation — The Stretching of Seconds

Imagine two identical, perfect clocks. You hold one. Your friend boards a spaceship, flies away at a significant fraction of the speed of light, then turns around and comes back home. When you compare the clocks, something unsettling has happened: your friend's clock has ticked *fewer* seconds than yours. From your point of view, time moved slower for your friend. Her seconds got stretched — dilated — like taffy being pulled.

And here comes the part that makes people accuse Einstein of madness: from *her* point of view, time moved slower for *you*. She saw your clock running slow the whole time she was traveling. Both perspectives are equally valid. Neither of you is wrong, and neither of you felt anything odd — your own heartbeat, your own thoughts, your own clock all seemed perfectly normal from the inside. The stretching only shows up when you compare notes afterward.

This effect is called *time dilation*, and it's not a trick of perception, bad clock-making, or a technological quirk. It is a feature of the *geometry of spacetime itself* — as real and structural as the fact that the angles of a triangle add up to 180 degrees on a flat sheet of paper but not on a curved ball. The exact amount of stretching is governed by the *Lorentz factor*, a number derived from Einstein's equations (named after the Dutch physicist Hendrik Lorentz, whose math Einstein built on).

Here's the practical scale of it. At everyday speeds, time dilation is minuscule — the universe hides it well. A commercial jet flying at 900 kilometers per hour experiences time dilation of roughly 0.0000003 seconds per year compared to a clock on the ground. In a year of nonstop flying, you would age about three ten-millionths of a second less than someone who stayed home. You'd never notice. But as speeds approach the speed of light, the effect stops being shy. At 99.5 percent of light speed, one year aboard the ship equals ten years on Earth. Speed is the dial, and the dial goes all the way to reality-bending.

And this is not theoretical. It has been measured directly, in the most everyday way imaginable: by putting extremely accurate clocks on airplanes.

**The Hafele-Keating experiment, 1971.** Physicists Joseph Hafele and Richard Keating flew four cesium atomic clocks — the most precise timekeepers in existence at the time — on commercial airliners around the world, first heading east around the globe, then heading west, and afterward compared them to reference clocks waiting at the U.S. Naval Observatory. Now, the planes' speeds were tiny compared to light, so the expected differences were measured in nanoseconds — billionths of a second. But atomic clocks are good enough to catch differences that small. The results, published in the journal *Science*, matched Einstein's predictions within the experiment's margin of error. The eastward-flying clocks, which were moving faster relative to Earth's center because they were flying *with* the direction of Earth's rotation, ended up having *lost* time compared to the ground clocks. The westward clocks, moving *against* Earth's rotation and thus slower relative to Earth's center, ended up having *gained* time. Both effects — opposite in direction — were exactly what relativity predicted. Time dilation was no longer a paper calculation. It was a boarding pass.

**GPS — the daily proof in your pocket.** Every time you use GPS for directions, you are using a machine that would fail within minutes without time dilation. Each GPS satellite orbits Earth at about 14,000 kilometers per hour and, because it is out in space, experiences weaker gravity than clocks on the ground. These two effects pull in opposite directions, and both must be accounted for:

- Because of the satellite's speed, *special* relativity says its clocks should run *slower* than Earth clocks — by about 7,200 nanoseconds per day.
- Because the satellite sits in weaker gravity, *general* relativity (which we'll meet in a moment) says its clocks should run *faster* — by about 45,900 nanoseconds per day.
- Add them up, and the satellite clocks *gain* roughly 38,700 nanoseconds per day — about 38.7 millionths of a second — relative to clocks on the ground.

That sounds like nothing. It isn't. GPS works by measuring tiny time differences between signals from multiple satellites to triangulate your position. An error of 38,700 nanoseconds a day accumulates into positioning errors of about *10 kilometers per day*. If GPS engineers did not pre-adjust the satellite clocks before launch to compensate — deliberately building them to tick slightly "wrong" so that they tick right once in orbit — your map app would place you in the wrong neighborhood, then the wrong town, then the wrong county, drifting further every single day. Modern civilization quietly leans on Einstein every time someone says "turn left in 200 feet."

#### The Relativity of Simultaneity — "Now" Is Personal

Time dilation is strange enough. But Einstein's famous train thought experiment reveals something stranger still: the very idea of two things happening "at the same time" is not absolute. *Simultaneity* — the relationship of happening together — is relative.

Here's the setup. Picture a very fast train moving past a station platform. Two lightning bolts strike the train — one at the front car, one at the rear car — at the exact same moment, as judged by an observer standing on the platform, exactly halfway between the two strike points. This observer sees both flashes at the same instant, knows she is equidistant from both ends, and concludes: *the strikes were simultaneous*.

Now consider an observer sitting in the middle of the train. She is also exactly equidistant from the front and rear of the train. But remember: the train is moving forward. So as the light from the front strike races toward her, she is moving *toward* it. Meanwhile she is moving *away* from the light of the rear strike. The front flash therefore reaches her eyes first. Since she knows she's equidistant from both ends, and since light travels at the same speed in all directions *for her*, she reasons: the front strike must have happened *before* the rear strike. For her, the strikes were *not* simultaneous.

And now the punchline: there is no umpire. Neither observer is mistaken. There is no higher court of appeal that can say who is "really" right. The question "did the lightning strikes happen at the same time?" has no absolute answer — it depends on how you're moving. Events that are simultaneous in one frame of reference are *not* simultaneous in another. And if simultaneity is relative, then there is no universal "now" slicing across the universe. Your present moment is not the same present moment as someone's who is moving relative to you — not because information is slow to arrive, but because the moments themselves are genuinely different. The Newtonian concept of one single present instant shared by all observers everywhere — one universal "now" — simply does not exist.

This is not philosophy. It is geometry. The mathematics of spacetime dictates that time and space are interwoven into a single four-dimensional fabric called *spacetime*, and different observers — depending on how they move — slice that fabric into "space" and "time" in different ways. Think of spacetime as a loaf of bread: how you slice it depends on the angle of your knife. Two bakers slicing at different angles get different cross-sections, and neither slice is the "true" one. Einstein showed that nature has no preferred knife angle. Your "now" is your slice — and it's as valid as anyone else's.

---

### 1.3 General Relativity — Gravity Is Curved Time

Special relativity, published in 1905, deals with a special case: observers moving at *constant* velocity — straight lines at steady speeds, never speeding up, slowing down, or turning. But most motion in the universe isn't like that. Planets curve around the sun. Apples accelerate toward the ground. In 1915, Einstein completed the picture with *general* relativity, and his central claim was even more radical than the first one: **gravity is not a force at all.**

That deserves a moment, because it overturns intuition we never question. Your whole life, you've felt gravity as a *pull* — Earth yanking you downward, keeping you in your chair. Newton described it exactly that way: an invisible force reaching across empty space, pulling masses together. Einstein said: no. There is no pull. What you feel as gravity is the *curvature of spacetime itself*, caused by mass and energy. Mass tells spacetime how to curve; curved spacetime tells matter how to move. Objects in "free fall" — including you right now, sitting still — are simply following the straightest possible paths through a curved geometry.

The classic comparison is a bowling ball on a trampoline. Roll a marble across the flat trampoline and it goes straight. Now set the heavy bowling ball in the middle: the fabric dimples, and the marble's "straight" path bends into a curve around the ball. It looks like the marble is being pulled by the bowling ball, but nothing is pulling it — the *surface itself* is shaped differently. Earth doesn't grab the moon with invisible hands; the sun dimples spacetime, and the moon rolls around the inside of that dimple like a marble spiraling in a funnel. (This picture is only a comparison — real spacetime curvature is four-dimensional and can't be drawn — but it captures the logic: the stage is bent, so the actors walk curved paths.)

And here is why this matters enormously for our story: **because time is part of spacetime, mass curves time itself.** The sun doesn't just warp the space around it — it warps the *time*. This produces a second kind of time dilation, called *gravitational* time dilation: clocks run slower in stronger gravitational fields.

**How much slower?** Picture two identical clocks. One sits at sea level; the other sits atop Mount Everest. The sea-level clock is deeper in Earth's gravitational field, so it ticks more slowly. The difference is tiny — the Everest clock gains only about 30 microseconds per year compared to the sea-level clock — but it is real, measurable, and predicted precisely by Einstein's equations. In 2010, researchers at the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) used extraordinarily precise optical atomic clocks to detect gravitational time dilation over a vertical distance of just **33 centimeters** — about the height of a one-year-old child. The clock on top ticked measurably faster than the clock below. Gravity slows time, and you don't need a mountain to prove it; you need thirty-three centimeters.

**Near extreme masses, the effect becomes extreme.** A clock orbiting just outside the event horizon of a black hole — the point of no return — would tick *infinitely* slowly as seen by a distant observer. From far away, time literally appears to stop at the horizon. If you watched an astronaut falling toward a black hole, you would see her motions slow down, her light redden and dim, until she appears frozen at the horizon, stuck there for eternity, never quite crossing.

But here is the profound anomaly — hold onto it, because we will meet it again: *from the astronaut's point of view, nothing unusual happens at the horizon.* She doesn't feel herself freeze. She crosses the horizon in a finite amount of her own time and continues inward toward the singularity — the point of infinite density at the center — where, for a black hole of stellar mass, she will be torn apart by tidal forces. The distant observer never sees her cross; the astronaut herself crosses without drama. Both descriptions are physically correct within their own reference frames. There is no God's-eye view from which the event happens "once, correctly." Time, at the edge of a black hole, is not one thing — it is a *relationship between observers*, and different observers genuinely disagree about what "when" means. Einstein's universe has no universal clock tower. Every observer carries their own.

### 1.4 Quantum Mechanics — Time as a Background Stagehand

If general relativity treats time as a dynamic, flexible dimension woven into the fabric of spacetime, quantum mechanics — the physics of the very small — treats time in a completely different way. And this mismatch is, as we'll see, at the root of the deepest problem in physics.

In quantum mechanics, time is not something you measure. It is not an *observable* — a quantity the theory allows you to observe, like position or momentum. Instead, time plays the role of an **external parameter**: a fixed background coordinate, supplied from outside the system, against which the system's quantum state evolves. The famous Schrödinger equation — the equation that tells you how a quantum system changes — reads, in essence: *the rate of change of the wave function is determined by the energy operator acting on the wave function.* Time appears in it as the variable *t*. But notice the asymmetry: position, momentum, and energy are each represented by *operators* — mathematical objects that act on the quantum state and correspond to measurable quantities. Time has no corresponding operator in standard quantum mechanics. Time is the stagehand, not an actor. It sets the schedule; it never appears in the play.

Now step back and feel the tension, because this is the whole problem in miniature. In general relativity, time is *dynamic and geometry-dependent*: it stretches, curves, and slows depending on mass, motion, and gravity — it's part of the furniture of the universe. In quantum mechanics, time is *fixed and external*: a rigid, unmoving backdrop that the universe dances in front of. These are two incompatible jobs for one concept. When physicists try to combine the two theories into a single theory of *quantum gravity* — a quantum description of spacetime itself — this disagreement becomes what is known, bluntly and famously, as **the problem of time**. As we'll see in Part 3, when you actually carry out the combination, time doesn't just get distorted. It vanishes.

---

### 1.5 Cosmological Time — The Age of the Universe, and Why Looking Out Means Looking Back

Every time you look up at the night sky, you are looking backward in time. This isn't poetry — it's simple arithmetic about speed and distance. Light travels fast, about 299,792 kilometers per second, but space is so unimaginably vast that even light takes years, millennia, or eons to cross it. And that means the light arriving in your eyes right now *left its source long ago*. The image of a star is a message, and the message has a postmark.

Get a feel for the postage:

- **The Sun.** Sunlight takes about eight minutes to reach Earth. The sunshine hitting your face right now left the sun's surface eight minutes ago. If the sun vanished *this instant*, you'd have no way of knowing for eight full minutes. The sun you see is always the sun as it was eight minutes in the past.
- **The nearest star.** Light from Proxima Centauri, our closest stellar neighbor, is over four years old when it arrives. You're seeing that star as it was when you were four years younger.
- **The Andromeda Galaxy.** At 2.5 million light-years away, its light has been en route since before *Homo erectus* walked the Earth. Every photon from Andromeda has been traveling since the age of the earliest stone tools.
- **The cosmic microwave background.** For the most distant light of all, the look-back time stretches to nearly the edge of the observable universe: about **13.8 billion years**.

That last one deserves the full story. The *cosmic microwave background* — usually just called the CMB — is the oldest light we can ever see: the afterglow of the Big Bang itself. Here's what happened. In its earliest moments, the universe was so hot and dense that it was a glowing fog — light couldn't travel freely because it kept colliding with the free-flying electrons of the primordial plasma, a bit like trying to see across a crowded room where everyone is tossing balls at everyone else. Then, about **380,000 years** after the Big Bang, the universe had expanded and cooled enough for electrons and protons to combine into neutral hydrogen atoms. With the free electrons gone, the universe became transparent to light for the very first time. The light that had been trapped in the fog was released all at once — and it has been traveling through expanding space ever since. Today we detect it as a faint microwave glow coming from every direction in the sky. It is, quite literally, the baby picture of the universe.

**How do cosmologists measure these enormous distances in time?** Their yardstick is *redshift*. As the universe expands, space itself stretches — and light waves traveling through space get stretched along with it, just as ink marks on a stretching rubber band would spread apart. Light waves that get stretched shift toward the red end of the spectrum (longer wavelengths), exactly like the drop in pitch of a passing ambulance siren, but for light. Cosmologists quantify this stretching with a number called *z*, the redshift: a galaxy with a redshift of *z* = 1 has had its light stretched to *twice* its original wavelength — meaning the universe has doubled in size since that light was emitted. The more distant the galaxy, the greater its redshift, and the further back in time we see it.

The current record holder for the most distant confirmed galaxy is a faint smudge cataloged as **JADES-GS-z14-0**, with a redshift of *z* = 14.32. Its light has been traveling so long that we see this galaxy as it existed only about **280 million years after the Big Bang** — when the universe was a mere 2 percent of its current age.

**And the age of the universe — about 13.8 billion years — where does that number come from?** It is not a guess. It is the point where multiple independent measuring sticks agree:

1. **The expansion rate of the universe** — the famous *Hubble constant*, describing how fast distant galaxies recede from us — lets you run the film backward to a hot, dense beginning.
2. **The temperature and pattern of the CMB** — its precise glow temperature (about 2.7 degrees above absolute zero) and the subtle mottled fluctuations across it — encodes the universe's age and composition.
3. **The abundance of light elements** — the precise cosmic proportions of hydrogen, helium, and lithium, forged in the first few minutes of the Big Bang — match the predictions of a universe of exactly this age and expansion history.

These three methods, utterly different in technique, agree to within a few percent. When independent yardsticks built on different physics all land on the same number, confidence is warranted: the universe is about 13.8 billion years old.

**One refinement, because precision matters here.** "The age of the universe" quietly assumes a particular definition of time, called *cosmic time*: the time measured by a hypothetical clock that has been at rest relative to the expansion of space since the Big Bang, and that has experienced the average gravitational field of the universe. That is a specific, idealized clock — and not every clock in the universe qualifies. An observer who has spent billions of years orbiting near a black hole, or racing through the cosmos in a speeding galaxy, has experienced *less* elapsed time since the Big Bang than the cosmic-time ideal. "The age of the universe" is not a single number stamped on every particle; it is a coordinate — a useful, honest convention that makes cosmology possible. Keep this in the back of your mind, because it foreshadows the deep debates of Part 3: even "the age of the universe" depends on whose clock you trust.

### 1.6 Penrose Diagrams and Conformal Time — Folding Infinity to Fit on a Page

To understand the *global* structure of spacetime — what happens at the edges of the universe, at the boundaries of black holes, and at the far limits of time itself — physicists use one of the most elegant tools in the field: the **Penrose diagram**, developed by Roger Penrose in the 1960s. (Penrose, incidentally, will reappear later in this book — in 2020 he received the Nobel Prize in Physics for his work on black holes, and in Part 2 he will offer us one of the most audacious theories of cosmic time ever proposed.)

The problem Penrose solved is this: spacetime is, in places, *infinite*. Light takes infinite time to travel to infinite distance; a black hole's future stretches forever; the far future of the universe may be endless. You cannot draw infinity on a finite page. So Penrose did something mathematically clever. He applied a *conformal mapping* — a special kind of stretching and compressing of distances, like projecting the entire globe onto a flat map — so that infinite distances and infinite durations are squeezed into finite regions on a two-dimensional diagram. Infinities get pressed onto the *edges* of the drawing, like the whole world pressed onto the rim of a circular map. What you lose in literal scale, you gain in something more valuable for this purpose: the diagram now shows the entire causal structure of a spacetime on a single page — *what can affect what, what can see what, and what regions are forever cut off from each other*.

The diagrams have a beautiful rule that makes them easy to read: **light rays always travel at 45-degree angles**, no matter how wildly the spacetime is curved. That's because the conformal transformation is designed to preserve angles. So if you want to know whether light (or any signal, since nothing outruns light) can get from event A to event B, you just check whether a 45-degree line can connect them. It's like a chessboard where the allowed moves of the fastest piece are always drawn the same way.

- In the Penrose diagram of *Minkowski spacetime* — flat, empty space — you see that light from any event can always reach "future null infinity" (the far edge representing the infinite future) and can always be traced back to "past null infinity" (the far edge representing the infinite past).
- In the Penrose diagram of a *Schwarzschild black hole* (a non-rotating one), the geometry shows its horrifying essence at a glance: once your worldline crosses the event horizon — the point of no return — *every* possible future path, even light's, bends inevitably toward the singularity. There is no direction you can point, nothing you can do. The diagram's lines all funnel to one point. Escape becomes as impossible as un-boiling an egg — not because of a force, but because of geometry.

**Closely related is the idea of conformal time**, used in cosmology. Here is the puzzle it addresses. In ordinary cosmological time *t*, the Big Bang sits at *t* = 0 and the universe expands forever into an *infinite* future. But switch to conformal time — call it η (the Greek letter eta) — and something magical happens for certain universes: the *infinite* future of an exponentially expanding universe (a so-called de Sitter space) maps to a *finite* value of η. Infinity becomes a number you can write down.

Why does that matter? Because it has profound implications for two of the deepest issues in modern physics. First, the **black hole information paradox**: an observer in such a universe can, in principle, receive signals from an infinite number of other observers, because the conformal diagram of the universe extends infinitely in space but has a *finite* temporal extent. Second, the **holographic principle**, which we will explore in Part 6: the suggestion that all the information in a volume of spacetime can be encoded on its boundary — like a hologram, where a flat film encodes a three-dimensional image. If the far future and far past of the universe are "finite edges" of a conformal diagram, then everything that ever happens might be writable, in principle, on those edges. Time, it increasingly seems, may be the kind of thing that can be mapped, folded — and perhaps, as we'll see later, even reconstructed from a boundary without it.

---

### 1.7 The Quantum Speed Limit — How Fast Can Anything Possibly Change?

Common sense says that if you want something done faster, you throw more energy at it. Press the gas pedal harder, and the car accelerates quicker. It would be natural to assume the same trade-off holds without limit for anything in nature. But quantum mechanics says otherwise. There is a **fundamental speed limit** on how fast any quantum system can change from one state into a measurably different state — a cosmic speed limit not on *motion through space* but on *change itself*.

This limit was first derived in 1945 by two physicists, Leonid Mandelstam and Igor Tamm. Here's the essence of what they proved, stated in plain language first: **the less certain a system's energy is, the faster it is allowed to change.** A quantum system whose energy is very precisely defined — one whose energy you could state to many decimal places — must evolve painfully slowly. A system with a big spread of possible energies can change quickly. In equation form, the minimum time τ (tau, the Greek *t*, the symbol for time) required for a quantum state to evolve into an *orthogonal* state — a distinctly different state — obeys:

τ ≥ πℏ / (2ΔH)

In words: the minimum time is at least the constant πℏ (Planck's reduced constant, the fundamental quantum yardstick) divided by twice the energy uncertainty ΔH. Bigger energy uncertainty ΔH → smaller minimum time → faster possible change.

Now, a careful reader who knows some physics may object: "Isn't this just Heisenberg's famous uncertainty principle, which says you can't know energy and time precisely at the same time?" And the answer is a firm *no* — and the difference is deep. Heisenberg's position-momentum uncertainty principle arises because position and momentum are both *operators* in quantum mechanics, and measuring one disturbs the other. But as we established in section 1.4, **time is not an operator** in quantum mechanics — it can't be measured that way at all. So the energy-time relation the Mandelstam-Tamm bound describes cannot come from the same math. Where does it come from, then? From the *geometry of Hilbert space* — the abstract mathematical arena in which all possible quantum states live. The bound is about how fast a state vector can *rotate* through that space given how much energy it has. Think of it like this: a state's energy spread is its turning radius. A precisely-defined energy is a very long lever arm — the state can only creep along its circle. A fuzzy, wide spread of energies is a short lever arm, and the state can whip around quickly.

In 1998, Norman Margolus and Levitin found a second, complementary limit, this one involving the system's *average energy above its ground state* (its lowest possible energy):

τ ≥ πℏ / (2⟨H⟩)

The combined limit — take the larger of the two bounds — is *tight*, meaning quantum systems can actually achieve it: it is not merely an estimate but the true ceiling. This is the ultimate speed limit on quantum information processing, and it has teeth:

- **Quantum computing.** The speed of quantum logic gates is fundamentally capped by this limit. You cannot make a quantum computer arbitrarily fast by simply pouring in more energy — the ceiling is set by quantum mechanics itself, through this bound.
- **Quantum tunneling.** When a particle sneaks through an energy barrier it classically shouldn't be able to cross (a phenomenon we'll meet again in Part 2, where it allows the universe to "bounce"), the tunneling time is governed by this limit.
- **Spread of quantum correlations.** How fast entanglement — that spooky quantum connection between particles — can be established or travel through a system is also capped by the quantum speed limit.

Later work has generalized the bound to *mixed states* (systems that are not in a single, pure quantum state but are statistically blended), to *open* quantum systems (ones interacting with an environment, which is every real system), and to systems with arbitrary types of evolution. These extensions matter enormously because no real quantum system is ever perfectly isolated — air molecules, stray photons, and thermal jiggling are always watching, in a sense — and the true speed limit must account for that constant environmental interference, known as decoherence (which we'll explore in Part 3).

The takeaway for our story about time: even *change* — the raw material of time's passage — has a floor and a ceiling set by nature's most fundamental constant. Time's flow isn't just relative; in the quantum realm, its *rate of production* is regulated by law.

### 1.8 The Quantum Zeno Effect — A Watched Quantum Pot Never Boils

There is an old saying: a watched pot never boils. In your kitchen, it's just impatience — the pot boils on schedule whether you stare or not. But in the quantum world, the saying is literally, physically true in a stunning way. **Continuously observing a quantum system can prevent it from ever changing state.** A watched quantum pot does not boil. This is the *quantum Zeno effect*, named after Zeno of Elea, the ancient Greek philosopher who argued that motion is impossible because an arrow must cross infinite halfway points (an argument that puzzled thinkers for two thousand years — the quantum effect borrows his name because it achieves a similar-sounding paradox: stasis through repeated interrogation).

Here is how it works. In quantum mechanics, an unstable system — say, a radioactive atomic nucleus — does not have a definite decay time stamped on it. Instead, it exists in a *superposition* of "undecayed" and "decayed" possibilities. Superposition is one of quantum mechanics' strangest ideas: until measured, the system genuinely occupies a blend of outcomes — like a spinning coin that is neither heads nor tails while it spins but a blur of both. For the nucleus, the probability of being found decayed gradually increases as time passes. Now — and this is the key move — imagine checking on the nucleus *constantly*, every tiny fraction of a second. Each check is a *measurement*, and each measurement forces the superposition to "collapse" to a definite answer: undecayed. The wave of probability that had been building toward "decayed" gets reset to zero. Check again instantly, and it resets again. If your checks come fast enough, the system *never* has time to evolve into the decayed state — because you keep collapsing it back to the start. The decay is *suppressed by observation*. The watched pot never boils.

This was proposed in 1977 by Baidyanath Misra and George Sudarshan, and at the time it seemed like a philosopher's fantasy. It has since been experimentally confirmed in real physical systems: trapped ions (single atoms held in electric fields), superconducting qubits (the building blocks of quantum computers), and ultracold atoms chilled to near absolute zero. The effect is not a trick of consciousness, and no mind is required — a mechanical detector watching the system does the job just as well. It is a genuine, physical consequence of the quantum measurement rule itself.

And nature has a sense of balance: there is also an *anti-Zeno effect*. Under certain conditions — depending on exactly how the observing interacts with the system and how often the checks come — frequent observation can *accelerate* a transition instead of suppressing it. The watched pot boils faster. Whether you get Zeno or anti-Zeno behavior depends on the details of the system's coupling to its observer (or measuring device) and on the timescale of the observations.

Why should you care about this beyond the wow factor? Three reasons, all of which echo forward in this book:

1. **Quantum computing.** Qubits — the quantum bits that store information in a quantum computer — must maintain their delicate superpositions long enough to compute. But every stray interaction with the environment is effectively a "measurement" that collapses the state. The quantum Zeno effect's darker cousin, decoherence, is the great enemy of quantum computers — and also, as we'll see in Part 3, a leading candidate for the very origin of the classical arrow of time.
2. **Quantum control.** Engineers have learned to *use* the effect deliberately: by measuring a quantum system at the right rhythm, they can stabilize fragile quantum states that would otherwise decay — holding a watched pot at a perfect simmer, forever.
3. **Foundations of physics.** The effect sharpens the deepest mystery in quantum mechanics: what, exactly, constitutes a "measurement," and what role does an observer — even a mindless mechanical one — play in the unfolding of physical events? If watching can freeze change, then the relationship between time and observation is far more intimate than common sense suggests.

---

### 1.9 Causal Set Theory — Maybe Time Is Nothing but a Web of "Befores" and "Afters"

Every approach to quantum gravity we've met so far tries to *quantize* spacetime — to take Einstein's smooth, continuous fabric and apply quantum rules to it. Causal set theory, developed largely by Rafael Sorkin and his collaborators, throws out the smoothness assumption entirely and starts over with a different picture. Its radical proposal: **spacetime is not continuous at all. It is fundamentally discrete — made of individual "elements," like atoms of spacetime — and the only relationship those elements have with each other is causality: which events come *before* which other events.**

Think of the difference between a photograph and a pointillist painting. In Einstein's picture, spacetime is a photograph — perfectly smooth, infinitely zoomable, continuous at every scale. In the causal set picture, spacetime is pointillism all the way down: tiny, indivisible dots of existence, with no smaller structure inside any dot. There is a minimum size to reality — at the *Planck scale* — below which you cannot subdivide space or time further. Trying to find structure inside a causal-set element would be like trying to find half a pixel.

In the mathematics of the theory, the smooth continuum is replaced by what is called a *locally finite partially ordered set* — "causal set" for short. The partial order is the heart of the idea: if element *a* precedes element *b*, then *a* could, in principle, influence *b* — a signal, a cause, a chain of consequences. If two elements are not ordered this way, they are causally disconnected — neither can affect the other, and for those two events, "before" and "after" have no meaning at all. Local finiteness means any finite volume of spacetime contains only a finite number of these elements — encoding the fundamental atomicity of space and time.

**A crucial technical virtue:** you might worry that making spacetime "grainy" would violate relativity's core rule that no observer and no frame of reference is special. Causal set theory threads this needle beautifully. The discreteness is "sprinkled" into spacetime in a *Lorentz-invariant* way — meaning that if you boost to any moving frame, the sprinkling still looks statistically identical, and no preferred reference frame is smuggled in. But this elegant solution has a strange side effect: the combination of discreteness and Lorentz invariance produces a characteristic *non-locality*. Events that appear spacelike-separated — far enough apart that no light signal could connect them in the smooth picture — can end up linked in the causal set. The theory whispers that the universe's causal web may be more subtly connected than Einstein's geometry suggests.

**And here is the theory's most striking claim — a genuine, risky prediction.** Causal set theory predicts that the *cosmological constant* — the energy density of empty space itself, the thing driving the accelerating expansion of the universe — should not have any particular fixed value. Instead, it should *fluctuate* randomly around zero, with a magnitude set by the discreteness scale. Now recall from section 1.5 that the universe is indeed accelerating — empty space does have an energy density, the cosmological constant, with a small but nonzero observed value. Most other theories of physics get this number catastrophically wrong — off by a factor of roughly 10^120, one of the most embarrassing mispredictions in the history of science. Causal set theory's fluctuation argument lands roughly in the right neighborhood. The "prediction" is controversial — critics argue it is not precise enough to count as a true prediction — but the fact that it is even in the right universe of answers, arrived at from such different assumptions, is intriguing.

On this view, time is not a fourth dimension at all, and it does not "flow" like a river. What we call time is the *accumulation of causal relations* — the before-and-after web woven one link at a time. Perhaps the most radical reimagining of time in modern physics: **time is not a container that holds events. Time is the pattern of befores and afters among events — nothing more, and nothing less.**

### 1.10 Stellar Time — The Clocks in the Sky

We end Part 1 where humans have always ended their days: looking up at the stars. And it turns out stars are not just beautiful — they are *time machines*, each one a window onto a different era, because when you look at a star, you are seeing a snapshot of a process that has been running for millions or billions of years. A star's light left its surface long ago, and the star itself is a slow-burning clock whose entire lifespan is written in its glow. Understanding stellar time gives us a profound perspective: in the cosmos, time is *inversely proportional to spectacle*.

**How a star lives.** A star's life is, at every moment, a battle between two opposing forces. Gravity pulls inward, trying to crush the star into itself. Pressure pushes outward, holding it up. When a vast cloud of gas and dust in space collapses under its own gravity, the material at the center is squeezed so hard and heated so hot that a remarkable process ignites: *nuclear fusion*. Four hydrogen nuclei (single protons) are forced together to form one helium nucleus — and because that helium nucleus weighs slightly less than the four protons that made it, the missing mass is converted directly into energy, according to Einstein's E = mc². This is the star's main sequence — the long, stable middle age in which it spends the overwhelming majority of its life, balancing gravity's squeeze against fusion's push, shining steadily. Our Sun has been on the main sequence for about 4.6 billion years and will remain there for roughly another 5 billion years.

**The astronomer's map of stellar lifespans: the Hertzsprung-Russell diagram.** The fundamental tool for understanding how stars age is a simple chart — the H-R diagram — that plots each star's *luminosity* (its total brightness, how much energy it pours out) against its *surface temperature* (which corresponds to its color, from hot blue to cool red, the way a stove element goes from dull red to blazing white as it heats). Plot thousands of stars this way and a striking pattern emerges: most stars fall along a diagonal band called the *main sequence*. Hot, bright blue stars sit at the upper left; cool, dim red stars at the lower right. Our Sun is a middling yellow star, appropriately in the middle. When a star exhausts the hydrogen fuel in its core, the balance breaks: the core contracts and heats, the outer layers swell and cool, and the star balloons into a giant or supergiant, moving to the upper right of the diagram — much brighter (because vastly larger) and redder (because cooler at the surface).

**The mass-luminosity rule — why the biggest stars live fastest.** Here is the mathematical heart of stellar time, and it contains a beautiful, counterintuitive truth. For a main-sequence star, luminosity grows *very* steeply with mass — roughly as the 3.5th power of mass (L ∝ M^3.5). A star's fuel supply is proportional to its mass (more star, more hydrogen to burn), but its *burn rate* is proportional to its luminosity. So the lifetime scales as mass divided by luminosity: τ ∝ M / M^3.5 = M^-2.5. Massive stars live much shorter lives. Run the numbers: a star ten times the mass of the Sun is roughly 10,000 times more luminous and lives only about 1/300 as long — around 30 million years instead of 10 billion. The most massive stars, hundreds of times the Sun's mass, blaze through their entire existence in just a few million years — cosmic mayflies, burning with a ferocity that is as brief as it is brilliant.

Flip the coin. The *smallest* stars — red dwarfs with less than 0.25 times the Sun's mass — burn their hydrogen so slowly, so stingily, that their lifetimes exceed the current age of the universe. They have not had time to evolve off the main sequence. If left undisturbed, these dim, unremarkable embers will keep shining for *trillions* of years — long after the Sun has swollen into a red giant, shed its outer layers, and cooled into a dark white dwarf cinder. They will be the last stars to die.

**The hierarchy of cosmic time.** Put it together and a profound temporal architecture emerges, almost a moral lesson written in starlight. The most massive, luminous, spectacular objects in the universe — the blazing blue supergiants that dominate any night sky — are the most *ephemeral*. The dim, humble red dwarfs that you'd need a telescope to see are the *eternal* ones. Grandeur buys brevity; modesty buys endurance. Time in the cosmos is distributed with an irony that poets would recognize: the brightest candles burn fastest, and the quietest ones outlast everything. When we reach the end of the universe in Part 8, it will be these unassuming red dwarfs — not the showy giants — that turn out the lights, trillions of years from now.

---

---

## Part 2: The Origin — Did Time Have a Beginning?

### 2.1 The Big Bang — The Conventional Beginning

Ask almost any cosmologist how the universe began, and you'll hear the same confident answer, backed by some of the most impressive evidence in all of science: about **13.8 billion years ago** (we counted the years in section 1.5), the observable universe was in an extremely hot, extremely dense state, and it has been expanding and cooling ever since. This is the Big Bang.

But before we go further, let's clear up the single most common misconception about it, because everything else depends on getting this right: **the Big Bang was not an explosion in space. It was an explosion *of* space.** Picture the difference. An explosion in space — like a firework — happens at a particular place, and debris flies outward *into* pre-existing surrounding space. The Big Bang was nothing like that. There was no "place" where it happened, and no "outside" for debris to fly into. Instead, *space itself* began to expand, everywhere, all at once. Every galaxy is not flying away from a central blast point; rather, the space between every pair of galaxies is stretching, like dots on the surface of a balloon being inflated (and in this comparison, the *surface* is the whole universe — the inside and outside of the balloon are not part of the story). Farther apart means stretching more, which is why distant galaxies recede faster — not because of any explosion's momentum, but because there's simply more expanding space between them and us.

Now, rewind the film. Run the expansion backward, and the universe gets hotter and denser as you go back:

- At the earliest moments, the *entire observable universe* — every galaxy cluster, every grain of dust, everything we can see — was compressed into a volume **smaller than an atom**. The temperature exceeded 10^32 Kelvin (that's a hundred billion billion billion billion degrees). The density was beyond anything we can replicate in any laboratory, now or in the foreseeable future. The four fundamental forces of nature — gravity, electromagnetism, the strong nuclear force, and the weak nuclear force — were merged into a single unified force.
- At about **10^-36 seconds**, the universe may have undergone a breathtaking growth spurt called *cosmic inflation* — an exponential expansion that stretched the universe from subatomic scale to macroscopic scale in an unimaginably tiny fraction of a second, smoothing out any initial wrinkles and stretching microscopic quantum fluctuations into the seeds that would later grow into galaxies.
- At about **10^-6 seconds**, the quarks — the elementary building blocks of protons and neutrons — were no longer free-flying; they combined into protons and neutrons.
- At about **3 minutes**, the universe was cool enough for nuclear fusion to build the first atomic nuclei: hydrogen, helium, and trace lithium. This is Big Bang nucleosynthesis, whose measured abundances we met in section 1.5 as one of the three pillars of the 13.8-billion-year age.
- At **380,000 years**, electrons and protons finally combined into neutral hydrogen atoms (the event that released the cosmic microwave background, also from section 1.5), and the universe became transparent to light for the first time.

It's an extraordinary story. But it has a boundary — and this is where the confidence ends. Run the equations all the way back to time *t* = 0, and general relativity itself breaks down. The density becomes *infinite*. The curvature of spacetime becomes *infinite*. Physicists call such a point a *singularity* — not a place, but a confession: a point where the theory itself fails, where its mathematics return nonsense. And at a singularity, the very concept of "before" loses its meaning. If you ask a cosmologist, "What happened before the Big Bang?" the honest answer is: **"General relativity cannot tell you. The equations do not extend to t = 0."** In Einstein's theory, time itself begins at the singularity — there is no earlier moment to describe.

This is not a satisfying answer, and it isn't meant to be. The singularity is a signpost, not a destination. It is the universe telling us — mathematically — that general relativity is *incomplete*: that at the extreme densities of the first instant, the quantum effects which Einstein's theory entirely ignores become dominant, and a quantum theory of gravity is needed. Every section that follows in this Part is an attempt to read that signpost and see what lies beyond it: a bounce, a smooth timeless beginning, a cycle without end — or, as some traditions have argued for millennia, no beginning at all, because every instant is a new creation.

### 2.2 The Planck Epoch — Where Spacetime Becomes Foam

How far back can we trust our story before the equations drown? There is a smallest meaningful unit of time in physics, called the **Planck time**: approximately 5.39 × 10^-44 seconds — a decimal point, forty-three zeros, and then a 5. It is the timescale at which our current theories of space, time, matter, and gravity must all be used together, because none of them alone can describe what happens. The Planck time isn't arbitrary; it's *built* from the three fundamental constants of nature — the speed of light *c*, Planck's constant ℏ (the quantum of action), and the gravitational constant *G* — in the only combination that yields a unit of time. When all three constants are needed at once, you know you're at the deepest level of physics.

Here's the problem in plain terms. In the first Planck time after the beginning, the energy density of the universe was so high that the *curvature of spacetime* — the bending of space and time by mass-energy — became comparable to spacetime itself. Einstein's equations assume spacetime is a smooth, gently curving manifold, like the surface of a calm lake. But at the Planck scale, the lake does not exist. In the 1950s, the visionary American physicist John Archibald Wheeler proposed what he called **quantum foam**: at this scale, spacetime is not smooth but *turbulent* — a violent, churning froth of virtual particles, microscopic wormholes tunneling through existence, and constant, chaotic changes in topology (the connectedness of space itself).

Where does the turbulence come from? From the uncertainty principle, applied to energy and time. Heisenberg's uncertainty principle says you cannot know both a system's energy and the time at which it has that energy with arbitrary precision — there's a built-in trade-off: the more precisely you pin down *when*, the more wildly the *energy* can fluctuate. Over very short intervals of time, the laws of quantum mechanics *permit* enormous energy fluctuations to pop into existence. And through Einstein's mass-energy equivalence (E = mc²), energy *is* mass, and mass *curves spacetime*. So these quantum fluctuations curve spacetime — and at ordinary scales, the positive and negative curvings average out to the smooth geometry we experience, the way individual wavelets on a stormy sea vanish when you view the ocean from an airplane. But at the Planck scale, you're down among the wavelets, and the fluctuations dominate completely. Spacetime itself becomes a frothing, churning sea with no well-defined shape at all.

The consequence for the origin of time is startling. "Before" the Planck epoch — if that phrase even makes sense — the concept of time as we understand it may not apply at all. Time itself may be an *emergent* property, like solidity: ice is solid because countless water molecules lock into a lattice, but a single molecule isn't "somewhat solid" — solidity simply has no meaning at that level. Likewise, time may have no meaning at the Planck scale, emerging only as the universe expands and cools enough for spacetime to settle into a smooth, classical geometry. On this view, **the origin of time is not a moment. It is a phase transition** — like water freezing into ice, where "solidity" appears at a critical point rather than existing all along. The question "what happened before time?" may eventually make as much sense as "what is the temperature of a single atom?" — a perfectly natural question that dissolves once you understand what the concepts actually refer to.

---

### 2.3 The Hartle-Hawking No-Boundary Proposal — Time Without a Beginning

In 1983, two of the most brilliant minds in theoretical physics — James Hartle, a pioneer of quantum cosmology, and Stephen Hawking, the legendary Cambridge physicist who would become the most famous scientist of his generation — proposed an answer to "what came before the Big Bang?" that is as elegant as it is strange. They called it the **no-boundary proposal**, and its claim, stated simply, is this: **the universe has no boundary in time — no first moment, no edge, no "before."** The Big Bang is not the beginning of everything. It is not a boundary at all. Time simply does not have a beginning in the ordinary sense.

How could that possibly work? To understand it, you need to know one curious fact about quantum mechanics: a particle does not follow a single path from here to there. Instead, quantum mechanics says it explores *every possible path at once* — the direct route, the scenic route, the ridiculous route that loops around the moon — and the probability of finding the particle at its destination is calculated by adding up contributions from all the paths. This is Feynman's famous "path integral" approach, and it works astonishingly well. Hartle and Hawking had a bold idea: apply the same logic to the *entire universe*. Calculate the "wave function of the universe" by summing over all possible geometries of spacetime — every possible shape the universe could have had, all at once.

But which shapes should count? Here is their decisive move. Hartle and Hawking proposed that only geometries with *no boundary to the past* should be included — geometries that are finite, closed, and smooth, with no edge, no wall, no starting point. And if you only sum over boundary-free geometries, something wonderful happens: the Big Bang stops being a sharp edge where time begins and becomes instead a smooth, rounded cap — like the *South Pole of the Earth*.

Let that comparison settle in, because Hawking loved it and it is genuinely clarifying. On the surface of the Earth, if you walk south, you eventually reach the South Pole — and then you *cannot keep going south*. There is no more south. Does that mean the Earth has an edge, a wall at the bottom? No. It means "south" is a direction that exists everywhere on the sphere but *stops being meaningful at the pole itself*. "South of the South Pole" is not a place that is somehow forbidden or missing — the question itself is malformed, like asking what is "north of north." Hartle and Hawking's proposal is that "before the Big Bang" is exactly like "south of the South Pole": not a forbidden region, not an earlier era, but a question whose words outrun the geometry they try to describe. Time doesn't start at a boundary. Time *emerges* from a boundaryless state.

The technical trick that makes this work is one of the most exotic-sounding ideas in physics: **imaginary time**. In the mathematics of the proposal, time is temporarily measured in units of the imaginary number *i* — the square root of −1, a number that exists on a perpendicular axis to all the ordinary numbers on the number line. It sounds like science fiction, but imaginary numbers are workhorse tools throughout physics and engineering. In imaginary time, the mathematical distinction between space and time disappears: the four-dimensional geometry of the universe becomes purely spatial — a smooth curved shape, like the surface of a sphere, with no time direction at all. The "beginning" of the universe becomes merely the point of maximum curvature of this timeless shape, smoothly rounded, not an edge. Then, as the universe expands and cools, real time emerges — the ordinary time we know, flowing in one direction.

**Now the honest caveats, because this remains a proposal, not an established fact.**

- It predicts that the universe began in a state of minimum size and maximum symmetry, which is consistent with the era of cosmic inflation we met in section 2.1. So far, so good.
- But calculating the wave function of the universe precisely is ferociously difficult, and different mathematical methods of calculation give *different results*. When your answer depends on how you do the math, confidence wavers.
- The proposal faces the *measure problem*: even if you have a wave function for the universe, extracting sensible probabilities from it — "how likely is a universe like ours?" — turns out to be notoriously ambiguous. Probabilities require comparing possibilities, and when the "set of all possible universes" is involved, defining a fair comparison rule has stumped the field for decades.
- Recent mathematical work, using the Picard-Lefschetz approach to oscillating integrals, has clarified some of the technical issues and shown which calculations were sound and which were artifacts — progress, but not resolution.

The no-boundary proposal remains one of the most influential ideas in quantum cosmology: a genuine, mathematically serious attempt to describe a universe where time does not begin at a singularity but arises smoothly from a timeless quantum state. Whether nature chose this route, we do not yet know. But it has permanently changed the question — from "what happened before the Big Bang?" to "what does it even mean for time to begin?"

### 2.4 Loop Quantum Cosmology — The Big Bounce

There is another route around the singularity, and it comes from a specific candidate theory of quantum gravity called *loop quantum gravity* (which we will meet more fully in Part 3, in the work of Carlo Rovelli and Lee Smolin). When the ideas of loop quantum gravity are applied to the whole universe — a marriage called **loop quantum cosmology**, or LQC — they produce one of the most satisfying results in modern cosmology: **the Big Bang singularity is resolved. There is no infinite-density point, because quantum geometry refuses to allow one.**

The key idea is *discreteness* — the same atomicity of spacetime we met in causal set theory (section 1.9). In LQC, the geometry of space itself is quantized: area and volume come in smallest possible units, like money coming in cents — there is no such thing as half a cent, and there is no such thing as a volume smaller than the minimum quantum of volume. At ordinary scales this discreteness is utterly invisible (a cent is a trivial fraction of the economy), but at the densities near the Big Bang, it changes everything.

Here is how the bounce works, step by step. In classical general relativity, a contracting universe is doomed: gravity crushes it toward infinite density and the singularity, full stop. But in LQC, as matter density approaches the *Planck density* — about 10^96 kilograms per cubic meter, the maximum density spacetime's discrete atoms can pack — the discreteness of quantum geometry produces an effective *repulsive force*. At that point, the collapse halts. The squeeze reverses. **The universe bounces.**

Picture a tennis ball falling onto a very hard floor. Classical physics says the ball can compress without limit as it hits — a mathematical singularity, density going to infinity. But the real ball is made of atoms: there is a smallest size, and at maximum compression the atoms push back. The ball rebounds. In LQC, spacetime itself is the "ball," and its quantum atoms push back at maximum density. What we call the Big Bang is therefore not a beginning *ex nihilo* — not a creation from nothing — but a *transition*: the moment of maximum compression in a universe that was contracting before it, and has been expanding ever since. Before the bounce: a previous cosmos, collapsing for billions of years. The Big Bang: the bounce itself, when quantum repulsion reversed the collapse. After: our expanding universe.

This isn't a mathematical parlor trick that works only for one idealized universe. The bounce has been shown to work for a wide variety of cosmological models — uniform ones, lopsided ones, even some with irregular lumps — and it is robust: different reasonable ways of doing the quantum calculations still produce a bounce. LQC also offers a natural mechanism for inflation (section 2.1): the quantum fluctuations present at the moment of the bounce can seed precisely the density ripples that later grow into galaxies and the large-scale structure of the universe. One process, two accomplishments: it eliminates the singularity *and* explains the seeds of cosmic structure.

**But a past-eternal bouncing universe faces a deep thermodynamic challenge.** Consider an infinite sequence of bounces — an eternal drumbeat of cosmoses, one after another. Here's the problem: each cycle leaves a residue. Entropy — the measure of disorder we will study closely in Part 5 — accumulates from cycle to cycle, like grime building up in a machine that is never cleaned. Heat death, the maximum-entropy end state, would eventually arrive, and a dead universe cannot bounce. This is the *Tolman entropy problem*, and it has real teeth. Moreover, a famous mathematical result known as the **BGV theorem** (after Borde, Guth, and Vilenkin) shows that any universe that has been expanding on average over its history — which ours has — must have had a beginning of sorts: a past boundary, not necessarily a singularity, but a first moment in at least one sense. Even bouncing models cannot be past-eternal without additional mechanisms to reset entropy between cycles.

So the Big Bounce gives us a universe where the Big Bang is a transition, not a creation — but it does not, by itself, give us a universe without any first moment. The question shifts once more: not "singularity or bounce?" but "what sets the rules for an entire sequence of bounces, and where did *those* come from?" As we are about to see, other approaches — and other traditions — answer that question in startlingly different ways.

---

### 2.5 Quadratic Gravity — A New Origin Story, No Quantum Gravity Required

Here is a surprising plot twist. Most attempts to fix the Big Bang singularity reach for quantum gravity — new physics at the tiniest scales, strings, loops, foam. But in 2026, physicists published a striking calculation showing that a modification of Einstein's *classical* equations might do the job all by itself. The theory is called **quadratic gravity**, and its premise is disarmingly simple: Einstein's equations might not be the final word on gravity, but just the first term in a longer mathematical story.

What does "quadratic" mean? Einstein's field equations relate the curvature of spacetime to the matter and energy it contains, in a perfectly linear way — curvature on one side, mass-energy on the other, like a simple balance scale. Quadratic gravity adds extra terms to the equations — specifically, terms proportional to the *square* of the curvature. Think of it as adding correction terms to a formula: like noticing that a bridge's sag isn't quite proportional to its load because the cables stretch in more complicated ways. These quadratic terms are utterly negligible at the curvatures we encounter anywhere in the modern universe — near stars, planets, even ordinary black holes. That's why we've never noticed them. But they become *dominant* at the extreme curvatures near the beginning of the universe — near the Planck scale — precisely where Einstein's equations fail.

And at those extremes, the quadratic terms change the story's ending. In standard general relativity, Stephen Hawking and Roger Penrose proved famous "singularity theorems": under very broad conditions — if the universe is roughly homogeneous (the same everywhere) and isotropic (the same in all directions) — a Big Bang singularity is *unavoidable*. You cannot escape it within Einstein's equations. But the 2026 calculation showed that once the quadratic terms are included, the singularity can be avoided after all. The modified equations allow for a **bounce**, or a smooth "beginning," rather than a point of infinite density.

Here is the beautiful part: the repulsion that saves the universe from the singularity arises from *classical geometry* — from the mathematics of curved space itself — rather than from quantum discreteness. Recall that loop quantum cosmology's bounce came from spacetime having minimum "atoms" of volume. Quadratic gravity achieves a similar rescue without any quantum discreteness at all: the extra curvature terms simply push back when the universe gets too dense, like a mattress that resists being compressed beyond a certain firmness. On this view, **the origin of time is not a quantum mystery but a classical transition.** The universe may have emerged from a pre-existing, contracting phase; or it may have *tunneled into existence from "nothing"* — where "nothing" means a state of zero size and maximum symmetry, not an empty void with properties, but the absence of a state altogether, like the absence of a wave before the ocean exists.

Two things are worth holding onto. First, quadratic gravity is not yet an established theory of nature; it is a candidate, taken seriously in part precisely *because* it offers this singularity-avoidance for free. Second, its lesson generalizes: the singularity at the Big Bang is less a wall and more a sign that our equations need extending — and several very different kinds of extension (quantum loops, classical curvature corrections, boundary conditions on the wave function of the universe) all seem able to build a door where Einstein's theory showed only a brick wall. Time's beginning, on all these accounts, is *emergent* — arising from a deeper state that is not quite "before" in any familiar sense.

### 2.6 Penrose's Conformal Cyclic Cosmology — Time Reborn from Its Own Death

Now for the most audacious theory of cosmic time in modern physics — proposed by Roger Penrose, the same mathematical physicist who gave us the Penrose diagrams of section 1.6 and who shared the 2020 Nobel Prize in Physics for proving that black holes are an inevitable consequence of general relativity. Penrose's **Conformal Cyclic Cosmology**, or CCC, dares to say something no modest theory would: *the Big Bang was not the beginning. It was not even a bounce. It was the far-distant future of a previous universe, transformed.* In Penrose's picture, the conventional history of our universe — 13.8 billion years from hot dense beginning to cold empty end — is but **one cosmic aeon in an unending sequence of such aeons**, stretching back without a first term and forward without a last.

Here is the idea, and it depends on a subtle insight about what the far future of the universe is actually like. In Part 8 we will walk through that future in detail; for now, the headline: if the universe keeps expanding forever, as current observations suggest, it eventually approaches a state called *heat death*. All matter decays. All black holes evaporate. The universe becomes a thin, cold, dark soup of photons (light particles) and gravitons (gravitational waves), expanding forever, nearly empty of everything. Maximum entropy. The end of all stories.

But Penrose noticed something strange about this end state. As the aeon winds down, all the massive particles — the stuff you could use to build clocks and rulers — have vanished. And in a universe with *no mass scale*, the laws of physics (at the level of massless fields like light and gravity) become *conformally invariant*: they lose all reference to size. A conformally invariant physics cannot tell the difference between a big thing and a small thing, a hot thing and a cold thing, an early era and a late era. Size and duration become meaningless. And if the infinitely dilute, cold, empty far future cannot be distinguished — by its own physics — from the infinitely dense, hot, compressed far past... then, Penrose realized, the heat death of one universe and the Big Bang of the next might be *the same state*, seen from two sides.

The mechanism: in the final state of each aeon, the universe's geometry can be *rescaled* — stretched or compressed uniformly, which is what "conformal" transformation means — so that the infinite future maps smoothly onto a finite, bounded "beginning." The future conformal infinity of each aeon joins, essentially smoothly, to the conformally stretched Big Bang of the next, across a spacelike three-dimensional surface called the **crossover surface**. The geometry at that crossover is conformally smooth — *except* at a discrete set of special points called **Hawking points**. Each Hawking point is the final signature of the single most massive black hole of a galactic cluster from the previous aeon — the moment, at the very end, when that black hole finished evaporating through Hawking radiation (which we will study in Part 7). These points are scars: the universe remembers its previous life through the ghosts of its dead giants.

Remarkably, Penrose and his collaborators claim to have found evidence. Hunting through the cosmic microwave background — the baby picture of our universe from section 1.5 — they identified circular patterns of slightly elevated temperature, "Hawking spots," that they argue are exactly the kind of fingerprint supermassive black holes from a previous aeon would leave. Controversially, they note that the apparent sizes of these spots match the sizes of the largest galactic clusters in our own universe, hinting that the physics of the previous aeon resembled ours.

The caveats: CCC requires our universe to have *negative* spatial curvature — to be "open," shaped like a saddle rather than a sphere. Current observations say the universe is extremely close to *flat*; Penrose counters that even a tiny negative curvature would suffice. And the claimed Hawking spots in the CMB remain disputed — other researchers have argued they are consistent with ordinary statistical fluctuations. CCC is, at present, a bold and coherent framework resting on suggestive but unconfirmed clues.

But consider what it would mean if true. Time would not be linear at the deepest level. Each aeon would have its own arrow of time — its own direction of increasing entropy and complexity — preserved within that aeon. But the *sequence* of aeons would be eternal: no first aeon, no last. Time would have no absolute beginning and no absolute end — only endless rebirth, each cosmos dying into the same boundary from which the next is born. In the words of the mythic traditions we will meet in Part 24, this is the eternal return, arrived at through the mathematics of black holes.

---

### 2.7 The Ekpyrotic Universe — When Worlds Collide

String theory — the leading candidate for a unified theory of physics, which proposes that the fundamental building blocks of nature are not point particles but tiny vibrating strings — offers yet another pre-Big Bang scenario, and it reads like the plot of an epic film. In this picture, called the **ekpyrotic universe** (from the ancient Greek *ekpyrosis*, the Stoic doctrine of the world periodically consumed by fire), our universe is a three-dimensional **brane** — the word is short for "membrane" — a sheet-like surface floating in a higher-dimensional space, rather like a slice of bread suspended in a loaf. Another brane floats parallel to ours, separated from us along a dimension we cannot perceive.

Now and then — over cosmic timescales — the two branes drift toward each other and *collide*. The collision is not a cosmic fender-bender; it is a release of staggering energy that heats both branes, flooding them with particles and radiation. From inside our brane, that flood looks exactly like the Big Bang: a hot, dense beginning, followed by expansion and cooling. Then the branes separate, our universe expands and cools, and the whole cycle slowly gears up to repeat. Each Big Bang is a collision, not a creation. Time extends infinitely in both directions, with no first collision and no last. Like CCC, it is an eternal return; but where Penrose's cycles are sequential lives of a single universe, the ekpyrotic cycles are more like two dancers who meet in an embrace, whirl apart for an age, and meet again — forever.

The ekpyrotic model has real virtues. The singularity problem dissolves: the collision is not a point of infinite density but a *finite-energy event* in a smooth higher-dimensional geometry, so the equations never blow up. And it naturally produces what observations demand: a nearly scale-invariant spectrum of density fluctuations — the specific pattern of tiny ripples in the early universe that seeded the galaxies. In fact, in its earliest versions it achieved this without invoking cosmic inflation at all, which was a major selling point.

But the challenges are equally clear. The scenario requires specific conditions in the unseen higher-dimensional bulk: a brane of negative tension, or a particular kind of bulk field, to make the branes collide and rebound in the right rhythm — and string theory does not naturally provide these conditions; they must be assumed. And like every cyclic model, it must face the entropy problem we met with the Big Bounce: why doesn't disorder accumulate from cycle to cycle until the whole system seizes up in heat death? Some mechanism — perhaps entropy being diluted across the collision, or the branes' dynamics resetting between cycles — must do the janitorial work, and no one has yet demonstrated that it does.

The ekpyrotic universe remains one of the most imaginative answers to "what came before the Big Bang?": not nothing, not a bounce, but *another world, brushing against ours*. Whether that collision is written into the geometry of reality, only future physics can say.

### 2.8 The Buddhist Doctrine of Momentariness — Time as Perpetual Creation

So far in this Part, every attempt to answer "when did time begin?" has been a physics theory: bounce, boundary, cycle, collision. But a very different tradition has been wrestling with this question for over two and a half millennia — and its answer is arguably more radical than anything in the physics literature. In classical Buddhist philosophy, the question "when did time begin?" is, in an important sense, **misposed**. Time cannot have a beginning, because time is not a container that events happen *inside*. Time *is* the events — the arising and perishing of each moment — and there is no time apart from them.

The doctrine is called **Kshanika-vada**, the Doctrine of Momentariness, and here is its astonishing claim: **nothing persists from one moment to the next. Everything that exists — every physical object, every thought, every sensation — is destroyed and recreated at every single instant.** The Sanskrit word *kshana* means "moment" or "instant," but it carries a deeper connotation than our casual use of "moment": it refers to the *continual decay of things*, the denial of continued identity to any part of nature, and the assertion that the universe undergoes a new creation at every instant. This is not a gentle statement about change over time. It is the claim that at every moment, existence is being destroyed and rebuilt from scratch — and the rebuilding happens so fast, in an unbroken chain, that we perceive continuity where there is only succession.

One of the most influential Buddhist philosophers, **Vasubandhu**, defined a *kshana* as an entity that possesses the nature of *coming into being and immediately vanishing, with no interval whatsoever*. A *dharma* — a momentary unit of existence, whether physical or mental — arises, exists for a partless instant, and then *perishes*, immediately giving way to the next momentary entity in the causal chain. The result is not chaos but a continuous flow — a *santana*, a continuum — of causally connected momentary events, like the frames of a film playing so rapidly that you see smooth motion rather than 24 still pictures per second. (Hold that comparison; it will prove surprisingly apt when we reach physics again in Part 3, where some modern theories — causal sets, the thermal time hypothesis — describe time as exactly such a succession of discrete events.)
